A Conversation With Ken Griffin

Citadel Securities(31.5K)·48:58·2026-10-23
Ken GriffinFounder, Citadel Securities
TL;DRCitadel Securities founder Ken Griffin warned that AI threatens white-collar jobs and faces potential investment overcapacity bubbles, while stressing that the technology also creates a golden era for entrepreneurialism and productivity.
He also cautioned that high U.S. government debt, tariffs, and government overspending threaten the economy unless offset by productivity-driven growth. Acceleration of White-Collar Capabilities: Griffin noted that AI models can now complete complex PhD- and master's-level...

TL;DR

Citadel Securities founder Ken Griffin warned that AI threatens white-collar jobs and faces potential investment overcapacity bubbles, while stressing that the technology also creates a golden era for entrepreneurialism and productivity. He also cautioned that high U.S. government debt, tariffs, and government overspending threaten the economy unless offset by productivity-driven growth.

AI Impact on the Future of Work & Corporate Strategy

  • Acceleration of White-Collar Capabilities: Griffin noted that AI models can now complete complex PhD- and master's-level tasks in hours that previously took employees four to six weeks.
  • Threefold Economic Emotions: Griffin experienced three emotions upon seeing AI's advancement:
    • Threat to White-Collar Jobs: High-level administrative roles are heavily impacted, making profound national reskilling an urgent necessity.
    • Corporate Competitive Moats: Incumbent companies face eroding competitive advantages, forcing them to innovate, lower costs, and increase customer value faster.
    • Golden Era for Entrepreneurism: Startups have a unique opportunity to generate employment and hire hardworking individuals regardless of their formal credentials.
  • Internal Corporate Spending on LLMs: Sophisticated corporations are becoming discerning about AI spending, using high-cost state-of-the-art models from OpenAI and Anthropic only where necessary while running other tasks on cost-effective open-source models.

Market Reset, Discerning Investors, and AI Bubbles

  • GPU Supply and Marginal Pricing Risks: Griffin compared GPUs to airlines, warning that if hardware provisioning exceeds demand, marginal pricing will go into free fall—paralleling the dark fiber oversupply during the 2000 telecom buildout.
  • Useful Life Reassessment: Investors are questioning the revenue models and capitalization rates of older chips, moving away from a blind thematic program of buying all data center infrastructure indiscriminately.
  • Presence of Bubbles: Griffin acknowledged that radical technological transformations are historically intertwined with bubbles (such as the internet boom and railroads), but leave game-changing market leaders in their wake.
  • Hedge Fund Deleveraging Fallacy: Griffin pushed back against the narrative that market sell-offs are driven by hedge fund deleveraging, calling it a convenient excuse that stops market participants from learning what other investors have correctly understood.
  • Situational Awareness Portfolio Trade: Citadel acquired a portfolio from Situational Awareness in under 24 hours to help them raise cash and protect year-to-date gains, managing the risk profile and trading out of key positions across several days.

Economic Policy, Fiscal Health, and Tariffs

  • Inflationary Tariff Impact: Griffin asserted that tariffs on Canada, Europe, and global allies have proven pro-inflationary and severed supply chains.
  • U.S. Fiscal Deficit and Crowding Out: Following pandemic-era spending comparable to World War II funding levels, the U.S. faces a $40 trillion federal deficit that drives up 30-year yields and mortgage costs.
  • Growth as the Primary Remedy: Griffin emphasized that the only path out of the deficit is growing the economy through productivity, innovation, and pushing European allies to reform restrictive labor laws.
  • K-12 Education Reform: Griffin highlighted failing public school systems—citing 53 schools in Illinois without a single student at grade level—and praised successful alternatives like Success Academies in New York.
  • Immigration Policy: Griffin advocated for providing brilliant foreign graduates of elite U.S. universities with green cards and creating a path for gainfully employed undocumented immigrants to remain contributing members of society.

Political Landscape and Leadership

  • Midterm Elections Check: Griffin views the upcoming midterms as a normal check and balance on the party in power, noting that the Republican House and Senate face severe challenges.
  • Partisan Support and Bipartisan Engagement: Griffin has supported both Republican candidates and Democrats like Rahm Emanuel and Barack Obama, encouraging his employees to back candidates who prioritize education, safe streets, national defense, and alliances.
  • Contrast with Socialism: Griffin warned that the left-wing model of high government spending (noting France's public sector spending at 60% of GDP and civil unrest) mirrors the nightmare conditions he witnessed in Chicago.
  • Critique of Illinois Leadership: Griffin criticized Illinois Governor J.B. Pritzker over pension reform and spending, accusing him of attacking taxpayers rather than addressing state fiscal addiction.

Geographic Strategy and Corporate Culture

  • Citadel's Center of Gravity: Citadel operates across a global footprint with headquarters in Miami, major employee bases in New York and London, and a leadership center of gravity located "somewhere over the middle of the Atlantic Ocean."
  • Miami Expansion: Miami benefits from pro-business conditions, safety, and a strong anti-socialist Cuban immigrant community, though it faces housing costs and traffic congestion.
  • Carnegie Mellon Robotics Partnership: A recent $3 billion gift to Carnegie Mellon University will establish a campus in Miami to foster a second industrial revolution led by robotics.
  • In-Office Collaboration: Citadel maintains geographic centralization within vertical teams because physical proximity and whiteboards are irreplaceable for developing young talent and driving rigorous debate.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Midterm Congressional Shift: Republicans will "almost certainly lose one or both houses or both legislative branches, the House and the Senate" in the midterm elections. — Ken Griffin

Notable Quotes

"Work that used to take our PhDs and masters 4 to 6 weeks to complete, high-level white-collar jobs, press the button on the keyboard, wait a few hours, there's the exact same answer." — Ken Griffin

AI Cyber Panic, Ken Griffin’s Bubble Warning, The Fall of France?

All-In Podcast(1.2M)·103:23·477.2K views·2026-10-23
Ken GriffinFounder, Citadel Nikesh AroraCEO, Palo Alto Networks
TL;DRTech leaders and analysts debated the economic reality of the AI boom amid market pullbacks and a massive global cyber vulnerability involving PHP, while also examining the roots of severe civil unrest and debt crises in France and the West.
The AI Bubble Warning: Citadel founder Ken Griffin warned that AI is almost certainly in a bubble, noting that companies are struggling to turn massive AI spending into measurable productivity. - Tech Stock Pullbacks: Tech markets saw a continued downturn on data center and...

TL;DR

Tech leaders and analysts debated the economic reality of the AI boom amid market pullbacks and a massive global cyber vulnerability involving PHP, while also examining the roots of severe civil unrest and debt crises in France and the West.

AI Market Sentiment and Diffusion Challenges

  • The AI Bubble Warning: Citadel founder Ken Griffin warned that AI is almost certainly in a bubble, noting that companies are struggling to turn massive AI spending into measurable productivity.
  • Tech Stock Pullbacks: Tech markets saw a continued downturn on data center and CapEx news, with CoreWeave down 10%, Meta down 6% after earnings, AMD down 5%, and SpaceX down 5%.
  • The Circular Startup Economy: Chamath Palihapitiya shared research suggesting 1% of companies represent 90% of AI token spending, and of that 90%, 80% comes from unprofitable VC-backed startups—creating a circular funding cycle.
  • Data on GPU Compute vs. LLM Traffic: Previewing unpublished Ramp data, Palihapitiya noted a rising blue-box trend where spenders are buying their own GPUs to shift toward open-source models rather than relying strictly on closed-source LLM traffic.
  • Enterprise Data Sovereignty: Large enterprises are altering buying patterns due to data sovereignty concerns, with major banks standing up entirely independent, ground-up data centers rather than relying solely on AWS or GCP.
  • Startup Signal vs. Enterprise Lag: Palihapitiya argued that startups rushing to spend VC money on AI is a strong signal of efficiency, whereas slow, cumbersome enterprises lag behind.

Enterprise Infrastructure and the Shift to Open Source

  • The Case for Open Source: Palo Alto Networks CEO Nikesh Arora (joining via the show description) explained that enterprises preference open-source and open-weight models because they allow organizations to modify weights, train models, and interrogate the chain of thought for regulatory explainability, auditability, and verification.
  • Inference Resiliency: Arora noted a shortage of global, resilient, high-availability inference providers outside the hyperscaler community, making it difficult for enterprises to utilize open source economically at scale.
  • Bare Metal Hybrids: Arora predicted that hyperscalers will eventually provide bare-metal access to let enterprises run open-source models, shifting profit pools away from pure chip makers.
  • Compute Scarcity: Arora emphasized that compute is highly dear and demand is insatiable, noting that even if every company embraced AI today, there is not enough compute in the world to satisfy the demand.

AI Cyber Panic and Software Vulnerabilities

  • The PHP Two Shell Exploit: Security researchers using AI discovered two critical zero-day exploits in PHP (which powers ~70% of websites like WordPress and Wikipedia), rated 9.2/10 severity by the PHP security team.
  • Chaos Post-Leak: Although fixes were slated for an October 22 coordinated release, leaks forced an early release on October 21, triggering millions of hacking attempts reported by Cloudflare and AWS, precautionary government website shutdowns across Europe, and NSA/FBI warnings.
  • Persistent AI Compute in Hacking: Arora explained that closed frontier models with immense compute and persistence can chain multiple vulnerabilities to find complex attack vectors faster than human security teams.
  • Virtual Patching: Security firms like Palo Alto Networks rely on perimeter defense products and virtual patching to block attackers at the firewall level when underlying systems run unpatched legacy software.
  • Agentic Banking Liabilities: Discussing personal agents logging into bank accounts, Arora and the panelists debated liability when AI agents trigger security flags or perform unauthorized actions, with Palihapitiya asserting that the user who grants the agent agency bears the liability.
  • Unconstrained Model Risks: The panel discussed the extreme risks posed by "obliteration" AI services that provide unconstrained models without guardrails, likened by Arora to handing out nuclear bombs.
  • Technical Debt & Maintenance: The panel agreed that accumulated technical debt and a lack of corporate prioritization for security maintenance leave systems perpetually vulnerable to automated AI-driven cyberattacks.
  • Cyber Budgets: Arora noted that enterprise cybersecurity budgets typically range from 4% to 10% of IT spend, with financial services and federal government sectors sitting at the higher end.

The Fall of France, Sovereign Debt, and Socialist Overload

  • Civil Unrest in France: Over 500,000 protesters took part in riots across France, Italy, Spain, and Albania, resulting in 3,000 damaged businesses, 4,000 injured police officers, 2,500 arrests, and the destruction of a 143-year-old city hall in Limoges.
  • Economic Indicators: France's 10-year bond yield surpassed 5% (highest since 2002), while Polymarket predicted a 91% chance of a Moody's or S&P credit downgrade by October 31, and an 18% chance of a state of emergency.
  • Public Spending vs. GDP: David Sacks highlighted research showing France's public spending as a percentage of GDP rivals historical communist regimes (Soviet Union in 1989, communist Romania, Venezuela, and China), with France spending heavily while growing GDP at only 0.5%.
  • The Role of Immigration: Palihapitiya and Jason Calacanis argued that unmitigated, unassimilated immigration from Northern Africa—combined with socialist welfare state promises—created a tinderbox of civil unrest and domestic terrorism.
  • Targeted Violence: The panel expressed outrage over rioters specifically targeting Chinese-owned small businesses and Jewish stores out of economic jealousy.
  • The Bond Vigilantes: Palihapitiya suggested that bond vigilantes could break the back of the French government by making its debt unfinanceable, forcing an economic reset.
  • Western Fiscal Parallels: Sacks warned that the U.S. faces similar fiscal cliffs with high debt-to-GDP ratios, bloated public spending, and unsustainable entitlement obligations.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Nikesh Arora: Predicts that hyperscalers will eventually provide bare-metal access to let enterprises run open-source models.
  • Nikesh Arora: Predicts that CISOs will become the next CEOs as every enterprise business function transforms with AI.
  • Chamath Palihapitiya: Predicts that France will lose the upcoming presidential elections and undergo a complicated reset of its bureaucracy and economy.
  • Polymarket data cited on show: 18% chance France declares a state of emergency by the end of the year.
  • Polymarket data cited on show: 91% chance Moody's or S&P downgrades France by October 31.

Notable Quotes

"The diffusion we need within AI is happening a little bit slower than we would all like... AI has a diffusion problem. It is too concentrated and ripping around in a small little ecosystem." — Chamath Palihapitiya
"If I can't validate, it's not going to be okay to go to your boss and say, 'I don't know what happened, but the IT guys put an AI model in there and it did it.' That's not explainable to the regulator in financial services and health care." — Nikesh Arora
"The more the government spends to try and provide more of those things, the worse those things get and the more unattainable they become." — David Sacks

AI Narrative Switched, Debt Yields Spike | The Weekly Wrap

Steve Eisman(227.0K)·21:59·157.2K views·2026-10-23
Steve EismanHost, The Weekly Wrap
TL;DRThe investment narrative has shifted from equity earnings to fixed-income math, as surging debt yields for AI neo-cloud companies like CoreWeave and massive capital expenditure plans threaten data center economics.
Meanwhile, major tech and industrial earnings presented a mixed picture—highlighted by Tesla's 0% revenue growth and Meta's heavy future infrastructure commitments—while the housing market remains locked due to high mortgage rates and soaring ownership costs. - French Debt...

TL;DR: The investment narrative has shifted from equity earnings to fixed-income math, as surging debt yields for AI neo-cloud companies like CoreWeave and massive capital expenditure plans threaten data center economics. Meanwhile, major tech and industrial earnings presented a mixed picture—highlighted by Tesla's 0% revenue growth and Meta's heavy future infrastructure commitments—while the housing market remains locked due to high mortgage rates and soaring ownership costs.

French Sovereign Debt and Global Markets

  • French Debt Pressures: Steve Eisman highlights that France's debt-to-GDP ratio stands at 120%, triggering a 16 basis point increase in the premium on French 10-year bonds over Germany to 175 basis points—the highest level since the 2011 European sovereign bond crisis.
  • US vs. French Debt Advantage: While US debt-to-GDP is also at 120%, the US dollar serves as the world's reserve currency and the global financial system functions on Treasuries, whereas investors are not forced to hold French sovereign debt.

The Shift in the AI Narrative

  • Fixed Income vs. Equity Mindset: Steve Eisman notes that while equity investors focus on stories, fixed-income investors focus on math, and the AI narrative has now shifted heavily to the debt markets.
  • CoreWeave's Climbing Debt Yields: The cost of CoreWeave's debt climbed from 9% to 15%, negatively impacting the entire AI complex.
  • Unworkable Data Center Economics: Neo-cloud companies like CoreWeave and Nebius lack strong balance sheets; raising future debt at a 15% yield makes the economics of AI data centers prohibitive, transforming parts of tech from capital-light to capital-heavy.

Broad Earnings Review: Tech, Autos, and Aerospace

  • Meta (Strong Revenue, Massive Commitments): Reported revenue of $64.6B (up 26% year-over-year) and a beat on earnings per share, driven by a 14% increase in average price per ad and the early success of its agentic AI Muse. However, costs jumped 68%, and the company agreed to spend approximately $825B in future leases and contracts mostly for AI computing infrastructure while having no positive cash flow.
  • Tesla (Stalled Growth & Zero Revenue Increase): Total revenues grew 0%. Automotive revenue fell 1% to $20.5B, energy generation and storage revenue dropped 16% to $2.9B, and EPS fell 4% to 48 cents, while services revenue rose 33% to $4.6B.
  • Netflix (Entering X-Growth): Revenue met expectations with 11.8% growth to $11.8B, but Q4 guidance came in below street consensus as streaming matures and YouTube captures newer media formats like short-form video and podcasts. Netflix also executed layoffs and replaced its Chief Content and Chief Film Officers.
  • SpaceX (Impressive Results Despite Massive CapEx): Total revenue jumped 161% year-over-year, driven by three divisions:
    • Space (Rocket Division): Revenue reached $1.8B (vs. $962M last year), nearly breaking even.
    • Starlink (Connectivity): Revenue rose to $5.2B (vs. $4.3B), with profitability reaching $2.4B (vs. $1.7B).
    • Superintelligence (SI / AI Division): Revenue jumped to $7.6B (vs. $2.5B) with positive profitability of $431M (vs. a $1.4B loss).
    • CapEx and Merger Concerns: Total CapEx hit $29.6B (up 185% quarter-over-quarter). Steve Eisman warns of massive future CapEx plans—including a $100B budget for Starbase Louisiana, Grok models, and data centers—and criticizes Elon Musk's disclosed committee discussions to merge SpaceX with Tesla.
  • General Motors & GE Aerospace: Both beat revenue and EPS estimates yet saw stock declines; GM grew revenue by only 2.9% and narrowed rather than raised guidance, while GE Aerospace grew revenue 18%, EPS 36%, and total orders 49%, but missed free cash flow expectations at $2B.
  • Equifax: Revenue grew 10% and US mortgage revenue rose 15% amid regulatory pressure from FHFA head Bill Pulte on credit scoring, though Q4 EPS guidance missed consensus by 3%.
  • Telecoms (AT&T and Moody's): AT&T reported EPS of 65 cents (up 20%) and revenue up 5.3%, but T-Mobile, Verizon, and AT&T shares fell 2–6% after Elon Musk reaffirmed intentions to build a fourth major mobile operator (coining the term "telepocalypse"). Moody's missed EPS estimates at $4.14 (vs. $4.27 expected) due to a 1% ratings revenue decline from slower bond issuance.
  • Other Notable Results: IBM beat on revenue and EPS with 5% growth; Blackstone saw EPS fall 13% to $1.32 with $63B in inflows offset by real estate write-downs; Comcast beat on EPS via Peacock and media but lost 144,000 broadband subscribers; NVR missed consensus on EPS ($89.45, down 20%) and new orders (4,391 vs. 5,043 expected) due to high mortgage rates.

The Housing Market and Home Buying Outlook

  • Locked Existing Home Market: Mortgage rates exceed 7% compared to 3% to 5% pandemic-era refinancings, meaning a buyer's monthly payment parity would require home prices to fall by roughly half—an event prevented by employment stability, leading to multi-decade lows in existing home sales.
  • Unaffordability Factors: Surrounding costs including home insurance, property taxes, and land-development fees make housing unaffordable.
  • Housing as an Investment: Steve Eisman argues that the era of homeownership generating wealth is over, predicting that housing prices will likely decline in most parts of the country for the next several years and advising buyers to purchase only for lifestyle reasons under the assumption of breaking even upon resale.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Steve Eisman predicts that housing prices will go down in most parts of the country for the next several years.

Amazon CEO Andy Jassy on the Infrastructure the AI Era Needs

a16z(304.0K)·60:39·2026-10-23
Andy JassyCEO, Amazon Ben HorowitzCo-founder, a16z Erik TorenbergHost, a16z
TL;DRAmazon CEO Andy Jassy explains how massive AI demand is driving the company's unprecedented $220 billion 2026 CapEx buildout, why lowering the cost of inference is critical for broad enterprise adoption, and how generative AI is fundamentally reshaping Amazon's internal development and product lineup.
Massive CapEx Scale: Amazon expects to spend roughly $220 billion in CapEx in 2026, a staggering sum that reflects the unprecedented pace of infrastructure expansion. Sold-Out Capacity: Andy Jassy notes that 2026 infrastructure capacity is sold out, 2027 is largely sold out,...

TL;DR

Amazon CEO Andy Jassy explains how massive AI demand is driving the company's unprecedented $220 billion 2026 CapEx buildout, why lowering the cost of inference is critical for broad enterprise adoption, and how generative AI is fundamentally reshaping Amazon's internal development and product lineup.

Amazon's AI Infrastructure & Capacity Demand

  • Massive CapEx Scale: Amazon expects to spend roughly $220 billion in CapEx in 2026, a staggering sum that reflects the unprecedented pace of infrastructure expansion.
  • Sold-Out Capacity: Andy Jassy notes that 2026 infrastructure capacity is sold out, 2027 is largely sold out, and a good chunk of 2028 is already committed.
  • The Cash Cycle & Visibility: Andy Jassy explains that data centers require capital investment roughly two years in advance with a 30-year useful life, while server and networking gear are purchased just months in advance based on clear demand visibility with an average 5-year deal structure.
  • The Barbell Demand: Andy Jassy describes AI demand as a barbell, noting that while massive frontier model labs are buying up massive capacity, mainstream enterprise production apps are still waiting for lower inference costs to scale broadly.

Custom Silicon & Annapurna Labs

  • Acquisition Genesis: Andy Jassy highlights the 2015 acquisition of Annapurna Labs—initially intended to offload virtualization, networking, security, and storage onto NICs to free up 100% of EC2 servers for compute—as one of Amazon's best-ever acquisitions.
  • Graviton Success: Andy Jassy shares that 99% of Amazon's top 1,000 EC2 customers use Graviton CPUs, which deliver about 40% better price performance than comparable X86 processors.
  • Trainium & Inference Costs: Andy Jassy explains that Annapurna's Trainium AI accelerators provide 30% to 40% better price performance, aiming to drive down the cost of inference, which he believes is currently an order of magnitude too expensive for pervasive enterprise rewriting of CPU applications.

High-Profile Investments & Partnerships

  • Strategic Investments: Andy Jassy addresses Wall Street's skepticism over Amazon's large investments in Anthropic ($8 billion) and OpenAI, framing them as key partnerships with two of the most successful category-defining companies.
  • Ecosystem Integration: Andy Jassy notes that Anthropic runs extensively on Bedrock and builds on Trainium, while partnerships with OpenAI allow customers to run OpenAI models on Bedrock via a unique stateful runtime environment designed for agentic workflows.

AI-Driven Organizational & Product Transformation

  • Radical Productivity Gains: Andy Jassy cites the Bedrock team rebuilding their inference engine with just 6 engineers in 76 days (a task that previously would have taken 40 people a year), proving that AI enables drastically smaller, cross-functional teams.
  • Workforce Shift: Andy Jassy predicts that organizations will employ fewer humans for routine coding, customer service, business intelligence, and legal work, shifting toward smaller, highly agile teams starting from much more advanced stages.
  • Alexa & Core Reinvention: Andy Jassy explains that Alexa's brains are being completely rewired from scratch with generative AI, and similar deep reinventions are underway across Prime Video, retail, Fire TV, and music rather than relying on incremental updates.

Cybersecurity Realities & Future Skills

  • Immediate Cybersecurity Threats: Andy Jassy warns that while media focuses on fantastical sci-fi AI risks, the immediate danger is cybersecurity—models uncovering vast new vulnerabilities that require applications to deploy patches in minutes rather than months.
  • Education & Critical Thinking: Andy Jassy advises young people (including his own children) to embrace AI tools rather than fight them, but stresses that human judgment, ingenuity, critical reasoning, and strategic thinking remain essential for synthesis and invention.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Andy Jassy predicts that supply constraints for various data center and chip components will persist for several years.
  • Andy Jassy predicts that companies will have to rewrite almost all of their code to be agentically capable and capable of responding to security threats in minutes.
  • Andy Jassy predicts that organizations in the future will employ fewer humans doing traditional tasks like coding, legal work, and research, shifting to smaller cross-functional teams.

Notable Quotes

"Even spending $220 billion in capex this year we're leaving a meaningful amount of demand on the table. ... 26 is obviously at this point sold out, 27 is largely sold out... A good chunk of 28 is sold out." — Andy Jassy
"The cost of inference has come down quite a bit in the last few years, but it's still an order of magnitude too expensive in my opinion for it to be used as pervasively as possible." — Andy Jassy
"If you want to think more than incrementally, you have to find a way to get teams to reimagine from scratch what those applications look like if you were building it today with all the new technology available like AI." — Andy Jassy
Andy Jassy said Amazon will spend $220 billion this year—and still won’t have enough capacity to meet demand. We always leave a certain amount for on-demand, but 26 is obviously sold out, 27 is largely sold out, but a good chunk of 28 is sold out. The financial markets are saying, oh, there's not enough money in the world to finance these things. Everything we do in the world today, stock market, banking, healthcare, they're all using data centers. Our job is to try to figure out how to have as much capacity as we can. We have an opportunity to help across so many more customer experiences and make their lives better and easier every day. The big labs, being willing to pay almost anything for power, cooling, chips. Well, maybe we should buy up the old guys and sell them to the new guys because they'll pay us whatever. Andy, welcome to the podcast. It's great to have you. Thanks for having me. So it's you're five years into running Amazon and you've done a lot being here and a lot is coming out as CEO. You know, right now, you know, $220 billion in capex spend, AWS growing 28% you know, there's a lot that you've you've achieved. How how do you think about the ambition in 2026 as it relates to Amazon, relative to 2021 when when you took over formally as CEO? Oh, 2021 feels so long ago. It feels like, you know, a decade ago. Yeah. But, you know, it's as you know, the world changes fast and, you know, if you compared to 2021, you know, our overall top lines, you know, about 80% higher. The AWS business, about, you know, almost three times bigger, our ads, revenue is about two and a half times bigger. So, the business has just, you know, grown quite a bit. And I think there are a few things that have really happened. You know, I think pretty quickly after I started in the role, which was on the tail end of the pandemic, in our retail business, we ended up with a real cost to serve challenge, where we had built, we had basically doubled the size of our fulfillment network that we had built over 25 years. We doubled the size of it in about 18 months, and then we built the last mile transportation network the size of UPS in about 20 months. And whenever you build that quickly, you end up with all sorts of things that you don't have optimized. And we our cost to serve in our fulfillment network were not what we wanted. And you know, it created a lot of challenges and angst, and a lot of people at that time, I remember, was just a year or so, or a little less than a year into starting the job, people said, "Boy, what a terrible time to start, and what bad luck, and" you know, I think in retrospect, it was really a great time to start and a and a and a real blessing because it allowed us to consider a bunch of things that we probably wouldn't have considered for many years, but they were really good ideas for our customers and for the business. And the biggest of which in our fulfillment network ended up being that we regionalized a full national network, a fulfillment network in the US. We regionalized it so we got items much closer to customers, which turned out to to both lower our cost to serve because with shorter transportation distances, while getting items to customers much more quickly. And that revaluation of our most closely held beliefs during that time, when we were struggling on cost to serve, really unleashed, speed of delivery, and a lot of demand in our retail business. Democratizing Security for the Agentic Web with Cloudflare CTO Dane Knecht

No Priors: AI, Machine Learning, Tech, & Startups(99.1K)·41:24·1.7K views·2026-10-23
Sarah GuoHost, No Priors Dane KnechtCTO, Cloudflare
TL;DRAs the agentic web drives an exponential surge in AI-powered security vulnerabilities and shifts the attacker landscape toward automated high school actors, Cloudflare CTO Dane Knecht predicts that engineering teams will spend the next 18 months heavily prioritizing security and zero-trust infrastructure to patch 70 years of legacy software.
Explosion of Vulnerabilities: Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs) and Log4J-scale incidents are shifting from annual occurrences to weekly events, driven by AI models and harnesses that do not sleep. Democratization of Offense: Dane Knecht noted that recent high-profile...

TL;DR

As the agentic web drives an exponential surge in AI-powered security vulnerabilities and shifts the attacker landscape toward automated high school actors, Cloudflare CTO Dane Knecht predicts that engineering teams will spend the next 18 months heavily prioritizing security and zero-trust infrastructure to patch 70 years of legacy software.

AI Security Landscape and the Evolving Threat Vector

  • Explosion of Vulnerabilities: Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs) and Log4J-scale incidents are shifting from annual occurrences to weekly events, driven by AI models and harnesses that do not sleep.
  • Democratization of Offense: Dane Knecht noted that recent high-profile vulnerabilities are not just the work of nation-state actors, but increasingly of high school students deploying agents in capture-the-flag contests and leveraging bug bounties.
  • The "Messy Middle" of the Web: Dane Knecht described the current internet as a messy middle where humans and agents ride the same rails, forcing websites into inefficient scraping and parsing cycles because agents have a thirst to explore the human-designed web.

Shifting Engineering Resources and Zero-Trust Infrastructure

  • Resource Realignment: Dane Knecht expects that over the next 18 months, the majority of engineering team time will be dedicated to security, with some team keynotes indicating over 50% of engineering time going toward securing the internet during this transition.
  • Zero-Trust Advantage: Organizations that transitioned away from castle-and-moat security toward Zero Trust over the past 5 to 10 years are in a significantly better position to secure devices and traffic flows.
  • Forcing Automation Through Crisis: Dane Knecht explained that Cloudflare plans to scale its internal red teams from one to four or five by the end of next year, forcing engineering teams to automate away vulnerabilities by pushing them out of crisis.

AI-Driven Workflows, Cloudflare OS, and Internal Experimentation

  • Cloudflare OS: Dane Knecht detailed Cloudflare's internal platform, which allows employees—particularly go-to-market and G&A teams—to safely deploy AI tools, build internal applications, and maintain data governance.
  • Bottom-Up Experimentation: Instead of hiring consulting firms to catalog workflows, Cloudflare fostered a bottom-up experimentation culture, utilizing intern energy and high-agency employees (including an early `agents@cloudflare.com` initiative powered by interns) to build hundreds of internal productivity tools.
  • Intelligent Tooling & Hiring: Dane Knecht emphasized that hiring at Cloudflare heavily prioritizes curiosity, drive, and empathy, noting that a lack of personal AI use at home is a major red flag.

The Agentic Web and New Economic Models

  • Cloudflare Monetization Gateway: Dane Knecht highlighted Cloudflare's new gateway designed to facilitate microtransactions for content creators, providing wallets for agents and stablecoin rails to handle millions of transactions per second.
  • Project Signals: Dane Knecht mentioned a collaboration with OpenAI called Project Signals, which allows agents to proactively discover new content rather than repeatedly crawling and paying for infrastructure across the entire internet.
  • Reviving HTTP 402: Dane Knecht expressed excitement that agents are finally pushing the internet toward solving the 30-year-old HTTP 402 payment protocol challenge, creating a new economic model that can increase the number of content creators.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Dane Knecht predicts that over the next 18 months, the majority of a team's time will be spent on security, after which software productivity will rise significantly as resources return to standard development.
  • Greg Brockman (cited by Sarah Guo) predicted that by 2027, people will spend as much money on tokens for security as they do for engineering.
  • Dane Knecht predicts a world in 18 months where new software productivity increases dramatically once security resources have surveyed and patched legacy bugs.
  • Dane Knecht claims that over time, cyber security models and tooling *all have to be open* because malicious actors will gain access to them regardless, and closing them excludes the mass population that needs defense.

Notable Quotes

"AI has become the tool that's causing this kind of security apocalypse is also the tool that can help us solve it faster as well." — Dane Knecht
"Right now we're patching 70 years of software out there, so some of it is just catch up. And then on a go forward basis, I think this software development life cycle, agent development life cycle is going to need to change." — Dane Knecht
"We're in the messy middle right now, where humans and agents are riding the same rails on the internet, where agents are trying to parse a human web, which is just inefficient." — Dane Knecht

NVIDIA GTC Berlin 2026 Keynote With CEO Jensen Huang

NVIDIA(2.3M)·91:59·2026-10-23
Jensen HuangCEO, NVIDIA
TL;DRNVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang delivered the GTC Berlin 2026 keynote, announcing a $10 billion European AI investment over the last year, the commercial rollout of the Vera Rubin architecture, the DSX Pod turnkey AI factory container, and OpenShell for secure AI agent runtimes.
The keynote also introduced breakthrough edge-to-cloud hardware, including the RTX Spark PC (1 petaflop) and DGX Station (20 petaflops) powered by the N1X chip, alongside multi-modal agent toolkits and physical AI robotics reference platforms. Longstanding Regional Roots:...

TL;DR

NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang delivered the GTC Berlin 2026 keynote, announcing a $10 billion European AI investment over the last year, the commercial rollout of the Vera Rubin architecture, the DSX Pod turnkey AI factory container, and OpenShell for secure AI agent runtimes. The keynote also introduced breakthrough edge-to-cloud hardware, including the RTX Spark PC (1 petaflop) and DGX Station (20 petaflops) powered by the N1X chip, alongside multi-modal agent toolkits and physical AI robotics reference platforms.

Europe's AI Economy and Industrial Evolution

  • Longstanding Regional Roots: Jensen Huang highlighted NVIDIA's 33-year history in Europe, noting that NV1, NV2, and NV3 chips were built in Europe, with early manufacturing partners in France (SGS Thomson, Croll) and Malta, and early workstation partners like Elsa (Germany) and Audi (2005).
  • Massive Financial Commitment: Jensen Huang reported that NVIDIA has invested nearly $10 billion across Europe in the last year alone into startups, major partnerships, and cloud infrastructure, bringing total European partnerships to over 12,000.
  • The Five-Layer AI Cake: Jensen Huang described the AI economy as a manufacturing framework composed of five distinct layers: Energy, Advanced Computers, AI Factories (generating token intelligence), Models, and End Applications/Startups.

Vera Rubin Architecture and Full-Stack Hardware

  • Generational Performance Leaps: Jensen Huang stated that through "extreme co-design" across chips, systems, software, and algorithms, NVIDIA has improved AI performance by 1 million times over the last 10 years, dramatically outpacing Moore's Law.
  • Vera Rubin Production Specs: Jensen Huang introduced the Vera Rubin architecture in full production, noting it delivers 30x greater performance than Blackwell in just two years, featuring 100% liquid-cooled nodes that assemble in under five minutes.
  • Networking and Silicon Photonics: Jensen Huang highlighted Spectrum-X, featuring Co-Packaged Optics (CPO) built with TSMC using CoWoS technology, yielding a 512-port 200 Gbps Ethernet switch with direct-to-chip laser integration.
  • DSX AI Factories & DSX Pods: Jensen Huang announced the NVIDIA DSX full-stack reference architecture and the DSX Pod—a containerized, factory-built AI factory integrating 7-rack Vera Rubin NVL72 units, 17 kilometers of Spectrum-X cabling, dry closed-loop coolers, and 800 VDC power centers, reducing on-site deployment from months to days.

AI Agents and Secure Runtimes

  • The Five Elements of an Agent: Jensen Huang outlined the core components required for autonomous AI agents: the Model (perception, reasoning, planning), the Harness (exoskeleton orchestrating memory and tools), the Sandbox (isolation layer), Knowledge Foundation (structured and unstructured data access), and Tools (CUDA-X accelerated solvers).
  • OpenShell Secure Runtime: Jensen Huang introduced OpenShell, an open-source (Apache 2.0) runtime that enforces system-call and network isolation for agents, paired with NVIDIA Sentry running on BlueField-4 for hardware-isolated trust domains that can cut compromised agent connections in milliseconds.
  • Enterprise Ecosystem Adoption: Jensen Huang noted widespread industry backing for OpenShell, including a major partnership with Anthropic to build cloud OpenShell agents.

Personal AI: RTX Spark and Workstations

  • RTX Spark PC Line: Jensen Huang unveiled the RTX Spark, a new line of desktops, laptops, and workstations developed over four years in partnership with Microsoft and powered by the N1X chip, delivering 1 petaflop of compute and 128 GB of memory in a portable form factor.
  • DGX Station Windows Workstations: Jensen Huang announced the upcoming availability (late this year) of the DGX Station running Windows, packing 20 petaflops and 754 GB of memory to run professional design software like CATIA, Autodesk, Adobe, Cadence, and Synopsys.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "Every single Telco in the future, with a hundred thousand sites representing some 100 gigawatts of distributed Telco base stations and data centers, will in the future evolve into a distributed AI network." — Jensen Huang
  • "The amount of idle computing... will reduce dramatically... the speed by which we can turn on, go from installation, arrival of computing to production of tokens will go from many months, we think something along the lines of 4 to 5 months down to about a month." — Jensen Huang
  • "Every single company in the future will use both closed models as well as open models that you customize." — Jensen Huang
  • "This reference robot will be available in the first quarter of next year, and it will come pre-assembled with computer inside, software integrated, and it should be able to perform quite well right out of the bat." — Jensen Huang (referring to the Agile One robot built with Agile Robotics)

Notable Quotes

"An AI a factory is not just software technology. It is software technology, but not just. AI, of course, runs on a computer, but the way that the computer operates is fundamentally different. Rather than a tool that's used by us, it is now part of a machinery, an infrastructure, factories that are producing intelligence." — *Jensen Huang*
"Today, the only thing stopping an agent from misusing all that is asking it nicely. Please don't delete anything, please don't push to main, please don't touch ssh keys. That's the please don't approach, but hope isn't security. Safety isn't an aspiration, it's technology and engineering." — *Jensen Huang*

Now before I talk about how to make the agents useful, the most important thing is we have to make sure that the agent is secure. The agent is autonomous, and it's autonomous system that has access to files, has access to resources, networks, access to tools, and we need to make sure that the privileges that we provide this autonomous system is minimal and that we constantly monitor it. We introduced a fantastic new product, a new platform, it's called OpenShell. OpenShell came just in time. This is a secure runtime that provides for the things that I just mentioned, and has an additional hardware-based monitor, before I explain it to you let's watch a video. Enterprises are preparing to deploy billions of AI agents. To be useful, they'll reason, plan, use tools, access data, and work together. Today, the only thing stopping an agent from misusing all that is asking it nicely. Please don't delete anything, please don't push to main, please don't touch ssh keys. That's the please don't approach, but hope isn't security. Safety isn't an aspiration, it's technology and engineering. NVIDIA OpenShell and Sentry put that philosophy into practice. You write a policy for what each agent can touch, and OpenShell enforces it at the granularity of system calls, file access, and network connections. Formal verification checks every policy change against your permissions ceiling, so agents never gain more access than intended. It's like the early Internet, viruses were everywhere, until browsers enforced strict limits on what a web page could do. OpenShell does the same for agents. Enforcement happens outside the agent, not just in LLM guard rails, bringing agent native security into your infrastructure. Every agent runs inside its own isolated sandbox with no direct route to the network. Every outbound network request goes through the supervisor outside the sandbox, it checks the action against your policy. If it's not permitted, it's denied, keys stay outside with the supervisor and are added only on the way to an approved endpoint. A compromised agent can't leak a key it never had. The gateway is the control plane, the supervisor makes the decisions, the sandbox holds the agent, and the policy prover checks proposed policies against your permission ceilings. OpenShell combines Linux kernel isolation with runtime policy enforcement, NVIDIA Sentry adds an optional layer of protection outside the host itself, away from the agent. Running on NVIDIA BlueField-4, Sentry monitors agent activity and enforces security policies in a hardware-isolated trust domain, because agent reasoning traffic passes through Sentry. If the agent is planning to do something unintended, it will update policies immediately and cut the connection to the model, quarantining it in milliseconds, and it keeps tamper-evident records that operators can verify for detection, investigation and response. OpenShell is open source under Apache 2.0, runs everywhere your agents do, edge to cloud, and takes two commands to adopt, install the CLI, create a sandbox, and run your favorite agent. Agents are only as useful as the access you give them, OpenShell and Sentry put that access under your control. That's how we advance AI capabilities and safety together. Agents are autonomous systems, we have to create extremely secure technology to make sure that we could develop these agents and deploy these agents safely. Now it's incredible, after announcing OpenShell, just about every major company in the world from the frontier labs to the operating system companies, cybersecurity companies, platform companies have come out to adopt OpenShell. It's really fantastic to see, and the reason for that is because this technology has arrived at exactly the right time, at a time when we wanted to deploy agents all over the world. Now remember, this isn't just about the services using OpenShell, as they deploy agents, this is really about all of the world's companies also adopting OpenShell, so that you could receive these agents and have them run autonomously inside your company, every single level of capability, every single task that you give the agent for every person that uses the agent, every one of those combinations requires a different level of privilege that you would provide to the agent, and every single time the end agent is launched, the privileges are different and so privileges, the policy enforcement, the monitoring all has to be done dynamically, and so OpenShell is really revolutionary. Palantir CEO Alex Karp on Life, Art, and Technology

Palantir(164.0K)·35:52·2026-10-23
Alex KarpCEO, Palantir Leon KalvaraGlobal Chairman of Banking, Citigroup
TL;DRPalantir CEO Alex Karp and Citigroup Global Chairman of Banking Leon Kalvara discuss AI sovereignty, institutional transformation, and the geopolitical AI race between the U.S. and China.
Karp emphasizes that Palantir operates like an "artist colony" with an exceptionally high bar for talent to build products that integrate complex institutional data while preserving enterprise intellectual property. Unusual Upbringing and Personal Journey: Raised in...

TL;DR: Palantir CEO Alex Karp and Citigroup Global Chairman of Banking Leon Kalvara discuss AI sovereignty, institutional transformation, and the geopolitical AI race between the U.S. and China. Karp emphasizes that Palantir operates like an "artist colony" with an exceptionally high bar for talent to build products that integrate complex institutional data while preserving enterprise intellectual property.

Background, Talent, and the "Artist Colony" Philosophy

  • Unusual Upbringing and Personal Journey:
    • Raised in Philadelphia by civil rights-active parents (an artist and a pediatrician), Karp developed an early skepticism toward power and orthodoxy.
    • Holds a philosophy degree from Haverford, a law doctorate from Stanford, and a doctorate in social theory from Frankfurt, alongside managing money for European families before co-founding Palantir in 2003 with Peter Thiel.
    • Identifies as severely dyslexic, a disability he credits with enabling faster, more creative, and honest processing of ideas outside standard distribution.
  • Palantir as an Artist Colony:
    • Described as an organization that seeks out "unusually jagged" N-of-1 talent with a flat hierarchy to manage them.
    • Focuses on converging unique societal-level problems with rare individual talents to build things no one else can construct.
    • Management challenge: Acknowledges that accepting and harnessing superior, highly creative talent induces psychological friction and institutional pain, but insists this tension is necessary to produce breakthrough technology.

Enterprise AI, Foundry, and Sovereignty

  • Core Technological Offerings:
    • Foundry: Integrates massive, disparate enterprise data sets.
    • Ontology: Acts as an application platform running ideas and operationalizing AI models across the enterprise.
    • Forward Deployed Engineers (FDEs): Serve as the critical transfer mechanism on the front lines, bridging the gap between cutting-edge technology and entrenched institutional clients.
  • Sovereign AI Architecture:
    • Allows organizations to power models they create and completely own, enabling them to white-label products or selectively share capabilities.
    • Impregnates tribal knowledge into the enterprise on a 1-to-1 basis, preventing institutional brain drain.
  • CEO Frustration and IP Protection:
    • Karp states CEOs are "livid" because they are paying for tokens that generate zero value while simultaneously leaking proprietary IP to competitors.
    • Sovereign AI restores control over true costs (combining cash price and IP loss) and actual value generation.
  • High-Stakes National Security Deployments:
    • Palantir software drives critical anti-terror operations in Europe and plays a substantial, highly-secured role supporting Ukraine and institutional transformations within the U.S. Department of Defense (such as Project Maven).

The Political and Wealth Challenges of the AI Era

  • The Wealth Disparity Crisis:
    • Acknowledges that massive wealth creation driven by AI—even for popular CEOs like Karp—cannot be easily explained to the American public or Western democracies.
    • Warns that failing to address this political reality plays directly into the hands of extremists on both the far-left and far-right.
  • Critique of Wealth Taxes:
    • Argues that extremist solutions like wealth taxes would crush startups, protect incumbents like large tech giants, and fail to solve the underlying political tension.
    • Surveillance State Expansion: Warns that assessing net worth down to granular real-time asset levels would require an intrusive surveillance apparatus, acting as a massive breach of civil liberties and individual privacy (e.g., exposing personal and financial transactions).

The U.S. vs. China AI Geopolitical Race

  • The Ultimate Tech Battle:
    • Emphasizes historical precedents (such as Japanese-Chinese history) showing that the country that wins the technological race dominates the world order.
  • Current Landscape and Capabilities:
    • Identifies the U.S. and China as the only two relevant players globally, describing Europe as lagging behind and largely forced to buy Western products.
    • The U.S. Advantage: Creativity and swashbuckling innovation.
    • China's Advantage: Massive scale, high-IQ population, and creative reactions to compute constraints (such as advanced model refactoring, distillation, and undercutting pricing).
  • Exponential Time Function:
    • Notes that the U.S. and China are lurching forward exponentially, with every single day of advancement widening the gap against all other nations by roughly 100 days.

Notable Quotes

"We look for people who are unusually jagged, and we... try to work to slot people and increase their intelligence where it's unusual, and that ends up with a very difficult to manage environment." — Alex Karp
"Cost is a metaphor for two things: What did you pay and what did you lose? The cost is not just what you paid for the token. It's what portion of your IP did you have to transfer to get the token?" — Alex Karp
"The country that wins this race will dominate how things happen very far down street... The battle space is us or China." — Alex Karp

This Is How You Should Actually Make Launch Videos

Y Combinator(2.4M)·47:09·25.9K views·2026-10-23
Farza MajeedFounder, Hey Clicky Aaron EpsteinHost, Y Combinator
TL;DRLaunch videos in 2026 no longer require expensive $20,000 commercial productions; instead, founders should treat every product demo as a mini-story and consistently ship raw, process-driven content on algorithmic social platforms.
Shift from Single Events to Continuous Shipping: Founders should abandon the outdated meta of coordinating a single, high-stakes launch (like a TechCrunch article or costly agency video). - Farza Majeed notes that a simple, authentic video saying "hi" and showing a product...

TL;DR

Launch videos in 2026 no longer require expensive $20,000 commercial productions; instead, founders should treat every product demo as a mini-story and consistently ship raw, process-driven content on algorithmic social platforms.

The Modern Launch Playbook: Mini-Stories Over Major Events

  • Shift from Single Events to Continuous Shipping: Founders should abandon the outdated meta of coordinating a single, high-stakes launch (like a TechCrunch article or costly agency video).
    • Farza Majeed notes that a simple, authentic video saying "hi" and showing a product performs far better than a $20,000 produced New York Times video.
    • Success comes from treating launches as "mini-stories" and constant demos rather than high-risk rocket launches.
  • Demo-Driven Development: Work backwards from a demo at every stage of product creation.
    • If a feature sounds good on paper as a video hook before building begins, it is worth creating.
    • The internal excitement of shouting *"Yo, check this out!"* to a co-founder is the exact raw energy that should be packaged for the internet.

The Content Creation Process: Scripting and Hook Mechanics

  • Religiously Scripting Off-the-Cuff Videos: Despite appearing natural, high-performing videos are meticulously planned in advance.
    • Farza writes a script days ahead, memorizes and visualizes lines in the shower or on the way to work, and uses text editors like *Freewrite* (freewrite.io) to refine the copy.
    • The core rule is evaluating the text against oneself: *Did I stop scrolling to read this sentence?*
  • The First 10 Seconds & Visual Hooks: Setup should comprise only 10-20% of a one-minute video.
    • Start with a clear hook defining who you are, what you are doing, and why it matters.
    • Utilize recognizable visual anchors in the first frame (such as *Xcode* for developers) and zoom in immediately to capture attention while users are scrolling.
  • Embracing the "Cringe" Phase: Getting past initial discomfort requires sheer repetition.
    • Farza admits that many produced videos initially felt terrible or "cringe" to his team before going viral (e.g., a video he thought was awful hit 3 million views on Twitter).
    • Founders must push through publishing imperfect content because audiences forget quickly and the reps build necessary taste and skill.

Channel Selection and Conversion Realities

  • Prioritizing Platforms by Down-Funnel Conversion: Vanity metrics (like total views) matter far less than actual user downloads and retention.
    • Twitter/X: Historically yields massive view counts (e.g., 3 million views) but features the lowest conversion rates for actual app downloads (less than 1%).
    • YouTube: Generates lower initial views (e.g., 20,000 views) but acts as a long-term library with extremely high conversion rates (roughly 20% download the app).
    • Instagram: Drives the majority of traffic (around 70%) for Farza's current company, *Hey Clicky*.
  • Targeting Niche Audiences on General Platforms: Mass-market algorithmic feeds can successfully capture B2B or specialized buyers if directly addressed.
    • By directly calling out specific personas (e.g., "Are you the founder of a design agency?"), Farza generated 50 targeted design agency leads through TikTok videos averaging 4,000 views.

Lean Production Stack and Team Structure

  • Essential Hardware and Software: High-end production is completely optional, but audio quality is non-negotiable.
    • *Screen Studio* ($30) is used for smooth screen and cursor recordings.
    • An iPhone selfie camera is sufficient for native, authentic updates.
    • For cinematic quality, Farza uses a *Sony FX3 / FX30* paired with a *DJI mic*.
    • Audio Warning: Clean audio is mandatory; viewers will tolerate 720p video in a basement, but poor audio causes immediate drop-off. Never use built-in MacBook or AirPod microphones.
  • Early Team Composition: Hiring for creative leverage early on frees technical founders to build.
    • Farza's first hires at *Hey Clicky* and *Buildspace* prioritized a filmmaker alongside engineers.
    • A dedicated filmmaker handles the execution (sets, shots, motion graphics, and editing), allowing the founder to focus on product, hiring, and growth.

Notable Quotes

"In order to not be cringe, you must first be cringe, so... you got to take out your camera and start posting some cringe stuff." — Farza Majeed
"The most important thing in any video you make... is audio quality. If your audio is bad, I'm bouncing." — Farza Majeed

Why Compute Still Trades in Group Chats — Affaan Mustafa & Alejandro Soumah Fernandez, Jump Trading

AI Engineer(863.0K)·20:37·4.8K views·2026-10-23
Affaan MustafaCompute Trading Lead, Jump Trading Alejandro Soumah FernandezCompute Trading Lead, Jump Trading
TL;DRMost GPU compute capacity still trades out of public view via private bilateral contracts negotiated over Slack, X, and Telegram, creating a financing squeeze for mid-size companies that must pay steep upfront prepayments.
Jump Trading’s compute trading leads Affaan Mustafa and Alejandro Soumah Fernandez explain how chips, power, capital, and evolving market structures drive this hidden "dark pool" economy. Unlit Market Dynamics: Most physical compute trades in blocks through long, bilateral...

TL;DR

Most GPU compute capacity still trades out of public view via private bilateral contracts negotiated over Slack, X, and Telegram, creating a financing squeeze for mid-size companies that must pay steep upfront prepayments. Jump Trading’s compute trading leads Affaan Mustafa and Alejandro Soumah Fernandez explain how chips, power, capital, and evolving market structures drive this hidden "dark pool" economy.

Market Structure & The "Dark Pool" of Compute

  • Unlit Market Dynamics: Most physical compute trades in blocks through long, bilateral commercial agreements—incorporating MSAs, SLAs, and hardware-specific order forms—rather than on widely adopted public exchanges.
  • OTC Channels: Tenured compute capacity for fixed terms and specific Total Contract Values (TCVs) is negotiated in private neo-chat channels, including Slack messages, X messages, Telegram group chats, and OTC venues.
  • Pricing Skew: Negotiations center heavily on the chip SKU and base board (e.g., H100, H200, B300) alongside memory types (VRAM, CPU memory, additional storage), delivery dates, and precise workload specifications.

Macroeconomics, Jevons Paradox, & Upstream Costs

  • Decoupling of Token and Compute Prices: According to a16z data referenced by the speakers, token and inference costs have fallen sharply while GPU compute prices have remained robust.
  • Jevons Paradox in Action: Cheaper tokens unlock vastly higher usage; teams consume significantly more tokens on the same budget, driving up underlying demand for compute.
  • Upstream Supply Chain: Bringing a usable GPU hour online requires the convergence of chips, power, buildings, grid connections, and massive capital.
  • IEA Electricity Projections: The International Energy Agency’s April update projects global data center electricity consumption to jump from 485 terawatt-hours in 2025 to 950 terawatt-hours in 2030.

The Middle-Market Financing Gap

  • The Hyperscaler Advantage: Hyperscalers, frontier labs, and large neo-clouds leverage strong equity, debt access, and large balance sheets to secure multi-year commitments (such as OpenAI's 10-year deals).
  • The Mid-Size Squeeze: Startups and Series B companies cannot easily underwrite $200M+ compute contracts over three years without eating an entire funding round.
  • Prepayment Hurdles: CoreWeave public filings show median prepayments of 15% to 25% upfront on cluster contracts; on a $100 million TCV cluster, a startup must put down $15 million to $25 million upfront, severely restricting operational runway.
  • Strategic Workarounds: Growing companies must weigh turning away customer demand, bridging shortfalls via spot/on-demand pricing, or financing the prepayment component against the opportunity cost of building and operating their own full stack.

Operating Models & Procurement Options

  • Build vs. Rent Spectrum: Companies can build and operate the full stack (handling power, permits, assembly, and management), own servers in a colocation facility with partner operations, or buy off-take rental capacity.
  • Control vs. Cost Trade-offs: Some businesses require root access, confidential compute, and topology customization, making full-stack ownership worth the premium despite rental alternatives.
  • Venture Capital Portco Impacts: VC investors increasingly focus on compute procurement because payment terms directly dictate portfolio company runway.

Market Participants, Brokers, & Policy

  • Ecosystem Roles: The market relies on price discovery providers (indexes and benchmarks for future derivative contracts), physical matching venues (decentralized marketplaces, bare-metal resellers, and neo-clouds), bilateral brokers, and specialist equipment lenders.
  • Bespoke Deal Complexity: Every deal features custom bills of materials, delivery lead times, and variable creditworthiness factors, making non-fungibility a major hurdle.
  • Export Controls & Open Weights: U.S. policy heavily shapes the market; in January, the U.S. Commerce Department moved qualifying H200-class license reviews for China and Macau to case-by-case review with conditions.
  • U.S. AI Action Plan: The plan explicitly encourages open-source and open-weight AI while recommending financial market improvements so startups and academics can access large-scale computing power.
